## E4 · Domain core

- [x] **PG-E4-01** `M` · deps PG-E0-01 · Core entities and value objects with validating constructors
  - Acceptance: Illegal states unrepresentable; construction failures are typed
- [x] **PG-E4-02** `S` · deps PG-E4-01 · `ExternalId` with provider prefixes and parsing
  - Acceptance: Round-trip property test over generated ID shapes
  - Verified: `external_id_round_trips_through_its_text_form` generates a provider and a value and asserts `parse(to_string(id)) == id`, and `external_id_parse_never_panics_on_arbitrary_input` covers arbitrary text. The `*(doing)*` marker was stale rather than hiding a gap.
- [x] **PG-E4-03** `L` · deps PG-E4-01 · `ReleaseName` parser: resolution, source, codec, HDR, audio, group, repack, season pack, multi-episode
  - Acceptance: ≥ 95% correct on a 200-name corpus; no panics on fuzz input
  - **A retraction stands here, and the defects found in its place are fixed.** A defect report that `Movie.2024.2160p.BluRay.DTS-HD-GROUP` loses its group was filed on this line and is **wrong** — that shape parses to `Some("GROUP")`, as do `DTS-GROUP`, `DTS:X-GROUP`, `E-AC3-GROUP`, `AC3-GROUP`, `TrueHD-GROUP`, `DDP5.1-GROUP`, `5.1-GROUP`, `HDR-GROUP` and `DTS-HD-GROUP[TRACKER]`. What a probe actually found were three narrower holes, all with one cause: the masker left a letter immediately before the group's hyphen, and `group_attaches` needs a tag boundary (`#`) or a channel-layout digit there. The fix is domain knowledge rather than a looser rule — `DTS-HD.MA` and `DTS-HD MA` are the profile's own name and `DTS-ES` is the matrix variant, so both are now recognized codec spellings mapped to the codec they name; bit depth is a tag the ranking does not read but a name still carries, so `10bit`/`8bit`/`12bit`/`hi10p` are masked without a field (`UNMODELLED_TAGS`). All three shapes now report `GROUP`, and the protection they must not cost is pinned: `Spider-Man.2002.1080p.BluRay.x264` still reports no group, because the rule reads the *last* hyphen and a title hyphen is followed by more tokens. The `PATTERNS` table's longest-first ordering is enforced by `pattern_table_is_sorted_longest_first`, which caught the first placement of the new entries.
  - Owed to **PG-E4-04**, not here: the release parser takes a release title by contract, so a filename's extension reaches the group (`…DTS-HD-GROUP.mkv` → `Some("GROUP.mkv")`). The filename parser knows where the extension starts and must strip it first.
- [x] **PG-E4-04** `L` · deps PG-E4-03 · `NameParser` for library filenames and folders
  - Acceptance: Same corpus requirement as PG-E4-03 plus anime absolute and `1x01` forms
  - Owed, reproduced by probe: the extension is extracted correctly (`…-GROUP.mkv` reports `extension: Some("mkv")`) but the **group absorbs it** — `release().group()` returns `Some("GROUP.mkv")` for `Some.Movie.2024.1080p.BluRay.x264-GROUP.mkv`. The cause is deliberate and worth keeping: the release tail is read on the whole text so that a tag in a folder name still counts, and `ReleaseName`'s group scan permits `.`. The consequence is not cosmetic: a library parse yields `GROUP.mkv` where a torrent release name yields `GROUP`, so group-based voting and dedupe see two different groups for one release. The fix belongs here rather than in the release parser, which is documented as taking a release title and is right to stop the group at nothing — this parser knows where the extension starts.
  - **Flaky property test, found and fixed after this task was ticked:** `a_generated_absolute_name_parses` generated title words from `[A-Za-z]{1,8}` and asserted that `"{words} - {episode:03}"` parses with that title and `BareAbsolute` numbering. But `ep` and `episode` are **episode-prefix markers** in this grammar, so the generated `"EP - 001"` is read as a marker plus an episode number and refused for having no title. The property was therefore false, and it passed only while runs happened to draw a word other than those two — it went green on four separate runs, including two full workspace runs, before failing here. The parser was right and the test was wrong: the generator now excludes the two spellings it can produce, and `a_marker_shaped_title_word_is_a_marker` asserts the refusal on purpose with the `PrefixedAbsolute` evidence and the number, so the ambiguous case is documented instead of left to a seed. Verified with 60 000 generated cases over three runs and no new regression seed.
- [x] **PG-E4-05** `M` · deps PG-E4-01 · `QualityProfile` model with rules, caps, and cut-off rank
  - Acceptance: Serialization round-trip and validation of contradictory rules
  - Settled: a duplicated entry in an accept list is now a contradiction rather than a harmless repeat — the second entry can never be reached, and a user who typed it believes they asked for something. `QualityRules::new` is fallible, the type deserializes through a draft that runs the same check (so a hand-edited profile file cannot carry a preference order the constructor would refuse), and `QualityProfileError::DuplicatedRule` names the list and the repeated value. The property generators deduplicate before building, or every generated profile would be invalid instead of exercising the ranker. Covered for all four lists, plus a round-trip test and a wire-form rejection built from a real serialization rather than a guessed literal.
  - Not this acceptance: `QualityRules` still cannot express an HDR preference list, but that is no longer a disagreement with the documentation — FLOWS F5 says HDR, seeder count and age are deliberately unscored because the profile has no input for them. `cutoff_rank` likewise still has no consumer in the ranker, because neither the profile nor `ReleaseName` gives a candidate a ladder position, so whatever implements upgrade/cut-off owns `release_decision.rank_position`.
- [x] **PG-E4-06** `L` · deps PG-E4-05, PG-E4-03 · `ReleaseRanker` pure scoring with per-rule reason output
  - Acceptance: Monotonicity, determinism, and totality property tests
  - **A second counterexample of the same class, found by the full workspace suite and NOT fixed here (PG-E14-17's round):** `crates/peregrine-domain/proptest-regressions/rank.txt` now carries a second seed, `Parts { resolution: UltraHd, source: Remux, video_codec: H264, audio: [Dts], group: Some("Hd") }` with `criterion = Audio`, and `a_strictly_better_position_strictly_raises_the_score` fails on it deterministically. **The scorer is right again, and the generator is wrong again, in a way the first fix did not cover.** `render` writes the audio tag and then the group immediately after a `-`, so `audio: [Dts]` with `group: "Hd"` renders `...DTS-Hd` — which the release parser correctly reads as **`DTS-HD`**, one position *above* `Dts` in the fixture profile. `improve` therefore computes `at` from the parsed set (`DtsHd`), sets `better.audio = [Dts]`, re-renders the *same* `...DTS-Hd`, and the score does not move. The trap recorded above is that a group can spell a codec **on its own** (`dd`); this is the adjacent case where a group **completes the tag before it** across the separator (`DTS` + `-Hd`, and by the same mechanism `DTS` + `-X`). The fix belongs here rather than to the scorer: either `improve` should verify its own premise — that `name_of(&better)` reads strictly better than `name_of(parts)` — and answer `None` when the group has swallowed the difference, which is exactly what its existing `return Ok(())` path already means, or `render` should separate the group from the audio tag so the two cannot concatenate. Left unfixed deliberately: it is this task's property and not the change that found it (`AGENTS R30`), and the seed is committed as `AGENTS §5` requires so the failure is permanent. **Not fixed, so the workspace suite is red on exactly this test.**
  - **Correction (found while running PG-E14-01's gates, resolved in round 12):** the monotonicity property this acceptance names failed on a seeded counterexample, and **the scorer was right** — the property's *generator* was measuring the wrong thing. `Movie.2024.2160p.REMUX.x264.EAC3-dd` states Dolby Digital Plus and, in its group, spells `dd`, which is how a release name writes Dolby Digital. The release parser reads a codec tag **wherever it appears**, so that candidate declares `[Eac3, Ac3]`, and `score_audio` takes the best position any of them holds — under the fixture profile, `AC3`'s, because that rotation ranks it above `EAC3`. The property's `improve` helper read the fixture's own `audio` list and asked for "one step better", which was a position the scorer was *already* holding, so the score could not rise and the assertion was testing a premise that was false. **The fix is in the generator:** it now measures the position the scorer sees, from `name_of(parts).audio()`, and the property keeps its full strength — a strictly better scored position still has to raise the score for all four families. **The seed stays committed** (`crates/peregrine-domain/proptest-regressions/rank.txt`); it is the case that now runs first and proves the fix. Two things were written down wrongly before the diagnosis landed, and the record is worth keeping: the first note said `improve` swapped the codec "to `Eac3`" (it swapped to `Ac3`), and the second blamed an implied-codec expansion in the parser (there is none — the expansion here comes from the *group* text). Both were guesses dressed as findings; what settled it was printing the parsed audio set and the two scored positions for the exact input rather than reasoning about them. The module doc's monotonicity sentence is now precise about what the scorer compares: the codecs the whole name mentions, not only the ones a caller passed in.

The four owed cells are in: `queued`, `inspecting`, `metadata`, and `seeding` now take `DownloadEvent::Removed`, `Event::download_edge` names the removal from all seven non-terminal states so `the_download_vocabulary_covers_the_machine` stays green, and `a_job_can_be_removed_from_every_non_terminal_state` walks `DownloadState::ALL` — asserting `Removed` from each non-terminal state and the terminal refusal from the other three — rather than repeating one assertion per cell.

- [x] **PG-E4-17** `S` · deps PG-E4-01 · `Clock` and `IdGenerator` ports for determinism in tests
  - Acceptance: `cargo xtask check-determinism` fails on a wall-clock or random read outside an adapter crate
  - The acceptance was **reworded, and the original wording was the reason this task looked closeable while its rule was broken.** It asked for "no `SystemTime::now` or random call outside an adapter" — and nothing in the tree uses `SystemTime` at all, so a grep for the string it named would have passed. What the tree actually had was `peregrine-sync` declaring its own **private production** `SystemClock` (`jiff::Timestamp::now()`) and `UlidGenerator` (`Ulid::generate()`) and wiring them into `SchedulerConfig::defaults()`, while `peregrine-platform::SystemClock` documented itself as "the one place that reads the machine's clock". One port, two production adapters, a shipping crate reading the wall clock outside the adapter, and a doc comment that was false. Both adapters are gone: the scheduler now takes `Arc<dyn Clock>` and `Arc<dyn IdGenerator>`, the real `UlidGenerator` lives beside `SystemClock` in the adapter crate with a deliberate `ulid` allowlist edge, and the `Default`/`with_defaults` constructors that could only exist by supplying an adapter are deleted.

    The gate is `cargo xtask check-determinism`, configured from `arch-allowlist.toml` so the patterns are data rather than code. It catches `Timestamp::now` and `Ulid::generate` — the two that mattered — and is pinned **not** to flag `Instant::now`, which is the monotonic clock, is what `tokio::time` controls in tests, and is what the scheduler's heap and the event ticker legitimately use. A negative fixture fails with exactly three named violations, and an empty `forbidden` list is a top-level error rather than a pass.
- [ ] **PG-E4-18** `M` · deps PG-E4-03, PG-E4-04, PG-E4-15 · Fuzz targets for release-name, filename, and subtitle parsers. **Its acceptance runs in Phase 4, and the toolchain is a decision the repository has not made.** The acceptance is a 24-hour run, and a libFuzzer harness needs a **nightly** toolchain — this machine has stable only, the workspace's MSRV is stable-minus-two, and AGENTS §3 lists no fuzz command, so adding `cargo-fuzz` plus a nightly requirement is a tooling decision rather than a task-level one. What already exists and what does not: all three parsers are fuzz-ready (each takes `&str` and returns a value, with no IO), and their no-panic property is *already* covered deterministically — `proptest` properties over arbitrary `String` and generated separator soup, plus a 200k hostile-input corpus for the filename parser. The residual is the harness itself: a `fuzz/` crate, one target per parser, and CI wiring. Writing the targets without a nightly toolchain would produce code that cannot be built, let alone run, so it is recorded rather than half-done.
  - Acceptance: 24 h fuzz run without panic in Phase 4
- [x] **PG-E4-19** `M` · deps PG-E4-17 · `Conformance`-friendly fake implementations for all ports
  - Acceptance: Fakes live in `testkit`, usable from any crate's tests
  - **"All ports" means both ports that exist.** `peregrine-domain/src/ports.rs` declares `Clock` and `IdGenerator` and nothing else. The previous doc comment in `peregrine-testkit` promised in-memory repositories and a scripted metadata provider as well; there is no port for either, so they are not here — a double for a trait that does not exist is the speculative work R3 forbids. The fakes are a frozen `FakeClock` (observable through `now_millis`, controllable through `advance`, the only thing that moves it) and a deterministic `CountingIds` (the Nth draw is `Ulid::from_parts(1_700_000_000_000, N)`, with `issued()` as the count the port itself does not expose). **The metadata provider is now the third port**: `FakeMetadataProvider` and `metadata_provider_satisfies_the_port` are here for it (PG-E5-01), so the promise this note recorded as not yet owed is paid, and `peregrine-testkit`'s own doc comment was corrected in the same change rather than left describing the old scope (R20).
  - **The conformance suite is `peregrine-testkit/src/conformance.rs`, and it is not a cycle.** `clock_satisfies_the_port` and `id_generator_satisfies_the_port` take the port method as a closure, so the same assertions run in `peregrine-testkit`'s tests against the fake and in `peregrine-platform`'s tests against `SystemClock` and `UlidGenerator`. The edge added is `peregrine-platform` (and `peregrine-sync`, `peregrine-app`) **dev**-depending on `peregrine-testkit`, which depends on `peregrine-domain`; no shipping crate gains a test dependency and no crate depends back on an adapter. The suite reads no wall clock, takes no lock, and sleeps nowhere — a suite that needed real time could not have been run against the fake. It asserts the floor that catches a unit mistake and that repeated reads never go backwards; it deliberately does **not** assert that two reads differ (a coarse clock is still a clock) and does **not** assert ULID ordering (two identifiers minted in the same millisecond order arbitrarily, which `UlidGenerator` documents).
  - `FrozenClock`/`CountingIds` are deleted from `peregrine-sync/src/tests.rs` and `peregrine-app/src/jobs/hello.rs`; their assertions are unchanged in strength. `peregrine-app`'s copy was **not** unused — it fed both `SchedulerConfig` and `HelloJob` in the two scheduler tests of that module — so what that deletion removes is duplication, not dead weight. `peregrine-domain/src/ports.rs` keeps its private single-use doubles for its own tests: `peregrine-testkit` depends on `peregrine-domain`, so a dev-dependency the other way would be the cycle.
  - **New allowlist edges, each deliberate:** `[crates.peregrine-testkit].runtime` gains `jiff` and `ulid` (the ports' own vocabulary, named in the fake signatures and in the suite; they cannot be dev-dependencies because a dev-dependency of an unpublished crate is not re-exported to the crates that run the suite), and `peregrine-app`, `peregrine-platform` and `peregrine-sync` gain `peregrine-testkit` in `may_depend_on` for the test-only edges above.
  - **Remaining — nothing blocking, but the phrase "conformance suite" is thinner than it sounds.** The suite is two free functions that each implementation's test calls by hand, so adding a third implementation means adding a call site; there is no test-attribute macro and no driver, because two ports and two implementations do not pay for one and a macro would want a proc-macro crate. When port three arrives, revisit this. Nothing in this task was verified against a second architecture or a second machine: the only proof that the production adapter passes is one local `cargo test -p peregrine-platform` run.
- [ ] **PG-E4-20** `S` · deps PG-E4-01 · Domain documentation pass: invariants stated where enforced. **The audit is done and recorded in [DATA_MODEL](./DATA_MODEL.md) §3:** all 39 invariants are classified as enforced with a citing test (I1–I4, I6, I7, I9, I11–I13, I28, I35–I39), enforced by tests that do not cite the identifier (I14, I15, I25–I27), a genuine gap (I10: nothing deletes a title, so the cascade rule has no enforcer until PG-E15), unenforceable because the path does not exist (I5 is guaranteed upstream by `TorrentInspector` refusing traversal before any row; I8 is vacuous because nothing writes `settings` yet), or owed by a phase whose tables no migration creates (I16–I24, I29–I34). **A first pass called I5 and I8 gaps and that was wrong** — the code path that could violate I5 does not exist and the control that refuses the bad input does, and I8's promised test would assert over an empty table. Also fixed: `RUSTDOCFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo doc -p peregrine-domain --no-deps` was red on two public docs linking the private `Event::name` and on `subtitle/mod.rs` linking `format`, which is both a module and a macro; it is clean now. **Remaining:** the acceptance asks for a named test per invariant, and that is unreachable for the 18 whose phases do not exist — so this task carries the same cross-phase block as the six E1 rows. The reachable work is to cite the identifier in the five suites that already enforce I14, I15 and I25–I27, to write I8's test when the settings registry lands (PG-E20-01), and to add I5's insert-time revalidation with the `download_file` writer.
  - Acceptance: Each invariant in DATA_MODEL §3 maps to a named test

# Phase 1 — Discovery

## E5 · Metadata providers

- [ ] **PG-E5-01** `M` · deps PG-E4-01 · `MetadataProvider` trait with declared capabilities and rate policy **Landed, and not ticked for one named reason.** `peregrine-providers` now holds the port: `MetadataProvider` (one operation, `search`), `ProviderDescriptor` with the `Capabilities` a provider declares, the `RatePolicy` and `CachePolicy` it answers under, the `Attribution` its terms require, and a closed `ProviderError` vocabulary. `peregrine-testkit` holds `FakeMetadataProvider` and `metadata_provider_satisfies_the_port`. The acceptance's first half is met, and the second is met for the port as it stands: the suite is pinned from both directions — the fake passes it, and four deliberately broken providers fail it (one answering with another provider's ids, one ignoring the limit, one claiming `search` and then failing, one refusing with the wrong provider's name). **What is not here is the other seven operations.** `ARCHITECTURE` §13 lists eight capabilities and all eight are declarable, but only `search` has an operation, so "a fake provider passes it" is true of a port that can do one thing. The rest return *drafts* of catalogue entities, and every domain catalogue type carries the `i64` row id only `peregrine-storage` assigns — so a provider cannot return a `Title`, it returns a draft, and the drafts are the normal form that PG-E5-06 to PG-E5-16 design against real payloads rather than against providers' documentation. **Four findings worth carrying forward:** the port is `dyn`-compatible by construction and that is tested, because the registry (PG-E5-12) holds providers of different types in one list and an `async fn` in a trait rules `dyn` out on this toolchain (`error[E0038]`, measured rather than assumed) — the operation therefore returns a boxed future, one allocation per request and no proc-macro dependency; `ProviderError` carries no free text at all, because TMDB takes its key as `?api_key=` and a `String` in the error is a route from a provider's failure message to a log tail a user copies out (SECURITY.md T13), so the detail is logged locally and the URL redacted; there is **no `Anilist` variant in `peregrine-domain::id::Provider`** although ADR 0007 and PG-E5-14 both treat AniList as an optional anime source; and ADR 0007 rule 6 requires conditional requests on every JSON endpoint "with `ETag` or `Last-Modified`" while `docs/research/data-sources.md` records that TMDB's JSON API has *no* documented `ETag` — `CachePolicy::revalidates` expresses either answer, and PG-E5-04 owns which is true.
  - Acceptance: Conformance suite defined; a fake provider passes it
- [x] **PG-E5-02** `M` · deps PG-E5-01 · HTTP client stack: rustls, HTTP/2, compression, timeouts, retry, circuit breaker **Unblocked and landed.** `reqwest` is already in `Cargo.lock` (0.13.5, pulled in transitively) but with *no TLS backend*: `rustls`, `native-tls`, `h2`, and `webpki-roots` are absent from the lockfile and from the local registry cache, and `governor`, `moka`, `backon`, and `reqwest-middleware` — the crates the `[crates.peregrine-providers]` allowlist already anticipates — are absent too. Enabling `rustls-tls` and adding the retry and rate-limit crates therefore needs a fetch that the agent environment refuses (the cargo registry lives outside the checkout), so this task had to be started by someone who could run `cargo add`/`cargo fetch`, which happened in the round that landed it. `wiremock`, which §13 names as the provider test harness, is absent for the same reason. **What can be done meanwhile, and was:** PG-E5-05's hygiene landed without a client, because a response is untrusted input whatever carried it. **The dependency fetch that this task was waiting for was authorised and run**: `reqwest` 0.13.5 with `rustls`, HTTP/2, gzip/brotli, JSON, and charset — and *no* default features, because `default-tls` would drag native-tls back in and undo the whole point; `wiremock` 0.6.5 as a dev-dependency, which §13 names as the provider test harness; and `tokio` for the retry wait. `backon` was added and then removed: the retry curve is a rule this crate tests at a deterministic instant (`Backoff::delay`), so the timing arithmetic is a few lines here rather than a dependency. The edge is recorded in `arch-allowlist.toml` — `check-arch` reports 110 crates now — and `THIRD_PARTY.md` was regenerated, because a new tree is new attribution. **`peregrine-providers::http` is the client:** one `reqwest` client per provider (built from that provider's policy, with redirects refused, because a redirect to another host would carry a request somewhere the caller did not ask about), and a retry loop whose whole policy is two pure functions — `Backoff::delay` for the curve and `is_retryable` for what is worth another attempt — plus `classify`, which turns a status into the port's own error. **A provider's `Retry-After` is obeyed and bounded at five minutes**, because honouring an untrusted header exactly is honouring an untrusted number. **The acceptance's three cases are named tests:** a `429` with `Retry-After` is retried and succeeds, a `5xx` is retried and succeeds, and a provider that never answers times out under the policy; alongside them, a `404` is *not* asked for again (retrying it spends a rate limit to be told the same thing), a provider that keeps failing is asked exactly `attempts` times, and the request carries a descriptive `User-Agent` (R24). **Two things the port already had were respected rather than worked around:** the error type carries no detail string — a URL can hold an api key, and this is the value that reaches a log — and it names the provider, so the client is built per provider and a build failure has an attribution. **The retry loop's first version did not consult `is_retryable`:** it retried whatever the curve allowed, so a `404` was asked for again, and only the `wiremock` fixture caught it — a tested function is not a used one. **Owed:** the circuit breaker the task names, and the global request-level concurrency cap that `PG-E5-03` defers to this client.
  - Acceptance: 429/5xx/timeout fixtures behave per policy
- [ ] **PG-E5-03** `M` · deps PG-E5-02 · Per-host token buckets and global concurrency cap **The per-host half landed; the concurrency cap did not, and for a stated reason.** `peregrine-providers::rate` holds `RateLimiter`: one token bucket per host, built from the `RatePolicy` a provider declares, with `reserve(host, policy, now)` answering how long the caller must wait and `park(host, policy, until)` remembering a `Retry-After`. It answers with a `Duration` instead of sleeping, which keeps the crate synchronous and object safe and keeps the scheduler the only async layer — and it takes the instant as a parameter, so `check-determinism` needs no exemption and a test drives ten seconds of traffic in microseconds. The acceptance is asserted directly: one call a millisecond for ten seconds against five a second with a burst of three allows between 50 and 53 requests. **Three arithmetic bugs were found by the tests written for those hazards, all three in the refusing path and all three the kind that silently exceed a provider's rate:** `unsigned_abs` on a negative elapsed time minted a million milliseconds of tokens for a clock that stepped backwards; truncating a non-integral spacing (90 a minute is 667 ms, not 666) let the refusing path answer `Duration::ZERO` at 666 ms, which every caller reads as "go now"; and a park that created the bucket empty spent the client's burst on the delay it was already serving. The spacing is now a ceiling division with a 1 ms floor on every refusal, elapsed time saturates at zero in both directions, and a park seeds a fresh bucket credited from the instant it ends. **Owed:** the global concurrency cap. The job-level cap already exists — `SchedulerClasses` bounds each job kind's in-flight attempts (PG-E6-03) — and what is missing is a *request*-level cap for one job that issues several calls, which needs an async "wait for a permit" and therefore belongs with the client that will call it (PG-E5-02). Building a synchronous `try_acquire` now would put the polling loop in every caller.
  - Acceptance: Synthetic burst never exceeds the configured rate
- [ ] **PG-E5-04** `M` · deps PG-E5-02, PG-E3-09 · Conditional-request middleware with ETag and Last-Modified to `provider_cache`
  - Acceptance: Repeat call transfers zero bytes (304 path asserted)
- [ ] **PG-E5-05** `M` · deps PG-E5-02 · Response hygiene: size caps, content-type check, validating decode, unknown-field tolerance **Landed ahead of its declared dep, and the dep points the wrong way.** `peregrine-providers::hygiene` holds `Body` and `BodyCap`: a provider's answer is size-capped, its media type checked when one was sent, and decoded into a validating model, with the three failure modes kept apart — over the cap is `TooLarge` and reports how large the body was, bytes that are not JSON at all (an HTML error page, a truncated read, an empty answer, non-UTF-8, or a structure nested 10 000 deep) are `Transport { Decode }`, and JSON that is not the expected shape is `SchemaDrift`, because "the provider changed how it answers" is a different fact from "we could not read what arrived" and ADR 0007 rule 7 keeps serving cache for the first. It takes `&[u8]` rather than a `reqwest::Response` on purpose: a provider's answer is untrusted input whatever carried it, and the rules about reading it do not depend on the client — which is why this task's declared dependency on PG-E5-02 is the wrong way round, and why it could land first. **The cap has no default here:** what is too large is a property of an endpoint, not of the port, so the adapter passes it exactly as it passes a rate and a cache policy. **The acceptance's "or marked stale" half is PG-E6-15's** — this module returns the typed failure and the sync layer is what maps a drift onto `MetadataState::Stale`. **Unexecuted:** the fixtures are byte literals in the module's tests rather than recorded responses, because the recording harness is PG-E5-17's and `wiremock` is not installed in this environment; the behaviours asserted are the ones §13 lists, but they are asserted against bodies this repository wrote rather than against what TMDB actually sends.
  - Acceptance: Oversized, HTML, and drifted JSON fixtures rejected or marked stale
- [ ] **PG-E5-06** `L` · deps PG-E5-01 · TMDB adapter: configuration, genres, trending, discover with the full filter set **The rails landed (PG-E7-08's slice):** the port grew `rail(RailKind, page)` — a vocabulary about *what a home screen can show* rather than a copy of TMDB's paths — and the adapter answers seven of them (`/trending/all/week`, `/movie/popular`, `/movie/top_rated`, `/movie/upcoming`, `/tv/popular`, `/tv/on_the_air`, `/tv/top_rated`) behind `Capability::Trending`, answering with `SearchCandidate`s so a rail item is exactly a stub title and the existing upsert path does the writing. Each endpoint has a `wiremock` test, the failure modes are pinned (401 `Unauthorized`, 500 `Transport`, drifted shape `SchemaDrift`), and `a_rail_the_descriptor_does_not_declare_is_refused_without_a_request` proves the capability gate spends nothing to find out. A rail item also carries `popularity`, which nothing reads yet. **Still owed:** configuration, genres, and discover with the full filter set — and with them the `title_genre` writes, since nothing in storage reads or writes a genre today. **Its first slice landed: `search`, the one operation the port has.** `peregrine-providers::tmdb` holds the adapter — the descriptor it declares, the client, the base URL, and the credential — and nothing TMDB-shaped leaves the module: the JSON types are private, they deserialize a *subset* of what the public API documents, and every field that could be absent is optional, so a person result, a missing id, a blank name, or an empty date is a result this build skips rather than an answer it refuses. **The credential goes in a bearer header, not a query parameter**, and that is a security decision rather than a taste one: a key in a URL reaches every log, every error, and any redirect that echoes it — which is also why this crate's error type deliberately carries no URL. `include_adult=false` is sent as well, because a provider-side filter is a courtesy to a household's search box while the *scope* decides what an account may see (`AGENTS R34`): different questions, and neither substitutes for the other. **The base URL is a parameter**, because an adapter that could only talk to the live service could not be tested at all (`R14`), and the tests point it at a `wiremock` server. **What the tests pin:** the mapping (a film and a series become candidates, a person does not, a blank name and a missing id are skipped, an empty date is an absent date), the limit, the bearer header, the kind choosing the *endpoint* rather than filtering an answer, the final statuses reported as themselves, and a drifted shape reported as a state rather than a panic or a half-built list. **Owed, and most of the task:** configuration, genres, trending, and discover with the full filter set — plus `PG-E5-07`/`PG-E5-08`'s details, credits, videos, and seasons. **Also owed:** running `peregrine-testkit`'s conformance suite against this adapter; it exists and the fake passes it, but the testkit is not yet a dev-dependency of this crate, and pinning the real adapter to the same suite is what `PG-E5-01`'s unticked half is about. **One finding for whoever writes the next fixture:** `wiremock`'s `set_body_string` sets its own `text/plain` content type and overwrites one set before it, so a fixture that "sent JSON" sent text/plain — and the adapter refused it, correctly, for three test runs while the fixtures looked right. `set_body_raw(body, "application/json")` is the call that sets both together. **What the sync will find missing, read from the storage layer rather than assumed.** `title_for_external_id` exists (`SELECT title_id FROM external_id WHERE provider = ? AND value = ?`, unscoped, on the read pool), `insert_stub_title` exists, `attach_external_ids` exists and refuses an id another title owns, and `reindex_title` exists with no production caller. **Three gaps, in the order they bite:** (a) **nothing updates a `title` row** — repo-wide the only `UPDATE title` statements are in `#[cfg(test)]` modules, there is no `WriteRequest` variant, and no write path exists at all for `title_alias`, `title_keyword`, `genre`, or `credit`, so the sync is the first writer of every provider field and must call `reindex_title` after writing any of them, because no trigger watches those tables (`fts.rs`'s module doc says so); (b) **`insert_stub` hard-codes `metadata_state = 'stub'`** and writes `created_at = updated_at`, so a title cannot be created already partial or complete, and `last_synced_at` is written nowhere today; (c) **there is no atomic find-or-create-and-attach**, and the failure mode is worth stating plainly rather than discovering: `title` has **no UNIQUE constraint**, so two concurrent syncs can both read `None`, both insert a stub, and only one `attach_external_ids` can win — the loser gets `StorageError::Conflict` and **an orphan stub with no id is left in the catalogue**, invisible to search by id and indistinguishable from a title the user has. The fix belongs with the first caller: one writer request that finds-or-creates by external id and attaches in a single transaction, so the row cannot be half-made. Recorded here because this is the task whose sync is that caller. **The seasons and episodes slice landed (`PG-E7-13`'s data, and the write side the title page was missing).** The port grew two operations in the `ProviderFuture` shape — `seasons(&ExternalId, TitleKind)` reading `/tv/{id}` and `season_episodes(&ExternalId, TitleKind, SeasonNumber)` reading `/tv/{id}/season/{n}` — both gated on `Capability::Season`, and **a film is refused with `Unsupported` rather than answered with an empty list**: an empty list is a real statement about a series, and the two draw differently. Storage grew the write half: `upsert_season_details` and `upsert_episode_details` are upserts on the schema's own `UNIQUE (title_id, season_number)` and `UNIQUE (title_id, season_number, episode_number)`, reachable only through two new `WriteRequest` variants, and `changed` is reported as whether a statement moved a value — the `WHERE` on the update is what makes that true, since SQLite counts an update that stored the value already there. **Numbering is never rewritten**: the numbers are the conflict key and no column the update writes, `season_id` is assigned once by the first writer, and `enrichment_never_rewrites_the_canonical_numbering` feeds an answer out of order and with a gap to prove a positional write fails it. `peregrine_sync::refresh_title` now fills a series in two more reads and answers with a `RefreshedTitle` carrying the pictures to fetch, because the blobs belong in the image cache and that layer has none (`R11`); a `404` on one season is that season's problem and its siblings are still written. The refresh job fetches the season posters and episode stills, best-effort where the title's own poster is not. **Still owed, unchanged:** configuration, genres (and with them `title_genre`), the full discover filter set, credits and videos, episode groups, and content ratings.
  - **The queried-list slice landed: `genres`, `browse`, and `similar`.** The port grew three operations the rail vocabulary could not express, because a rail takes no arguments: `genres(TitleKind)` reads a provider's filter vocabulary (`/genre/movie/list` and `/genre/tv/list` — separate endpoints because the two number even the names they share differently, which is why the kind is a parameter), `browse(&BrowseRequest)` reads one page of a list a *caller* composes (`/discover/movie|tv` with `sort_by`, `with_genres`, a score floor, and a page) and answers a `ProviderPage` rather than a bare list so "is there more" is the adapter's answer instead of a screen's arithmetic, and `similar(&ExternalId, TitleKind)` reads what a provider lists alongside one title (`/movie|tv/{id}/similar`). All three are gated on the existing `Capability::Trending` rather than a ninth capability: `ARCHITECTURE` §13 fixes the set at eight, and "read what is popular now" is the one that describes a provider publishing queried lists at all — a provider that declares it and has no such endpoint refuses with `Unsupported`, which is the port's own rule. The TMDB order mapping is total in both order and kind because the two kinds do not share a date field, and **sorting by score sends a vote floor** (`RATING_VOTE_FLOOR`), because TMDB orders by the raw average and the unfiltered page is a wall of `10.0` from one vote. The conformance suite grew the matching probes — a page no larger than a page, an empty page that may not claim another, an unnamable or repeated genre id, an over-long recommendation page, and every refusal naming the provider that made it — each with a test that fails a provider breaking exactly that rule. **Still owed from this task's own list:** configuration (the size list and its caching) and the remaining filter dimensions (year, runtime, language, country, provider, status); and the adapter is still not pinned to the conformance suite as a dev-dependency, which is `PG-E5-01`'s unticked half.
  - Acceptance: Fixture replay produces identical normalized output
- [ ] **PG-E5-07** `M` · deps PG-E5-06 · TMDB adapter: movie details, credits, videos, external ids, release dates **Its first slice landed: the port grew a second operation and a stub can now be filled.** `MetadataProvider::title(&ExternalId, TitleKind)` reads one title's details; `TitleDetails` (domain) is what a provider said and nothing about the database, so every field is optional and **an absent field never clears a stored one** — the update writes `COALESCE(?, column)` for all of them, because a provider drops fields between releases and the sync cannot tell an omission from a value. The adapter maps TMDB's film object into it (`/movie/{id}`), and `peregrine-sync::refresh_title` fills a stub from whichever provider already holds an id for it, moving `metadata_state` to `partial`. **The kind is a parameter of `title`, not taken from the id**, because TMDB numbers films and series in one namespace — an id alone does not say which endpoint answers it. **The storage half is new too:** nothing updated a `title` row before this, and `update_title_metadata` rewrites the search index in the same transaction, since `title`'s columns feed it and no trigger watches the table. **Owed:** series details, credits, videos, and external ids (`/tv/{id}`, `append_to_response`), the `status` and `content_rating` mappings (which need TMDB's `release_dates`), `origin_country` for films (its shape there is unverified), and artwork variants and blurhashes (`PG-E5-15`).

- [ ] **PG-E5-12** `S` · deps PG-E5-01 · Provider registry manifest: enablement, priority, auth requirement, endpoints **The manifest and the credential lookup landed; user enablement and priority are what is still owed.** `peregrine-app::providers` holds `MANIFEST` — one entry per adapter that exists, with whether it needs a credential and what endpoint it defaults to — plus `status(store)` and `build(entry, store)`, which read the keyring port and either report a provider as configured or construct its adapter. **A provider with no credential is *known* and not *configured***, and that distinction is the one the settings screen and the health check both need: nobody has set a key up is not an error, and a request to one is never attempted. **The one thing this round taught, at the cost of a red gate:** the first version read the keyring inside `Runtime::health`, and the existing command-budget test (`system::health` must answer inside 100 ms — `ARCHITECTURE §7` rule 2) failed at **11.7 seconds**, because the first keyring call from a new binary raises an OS prompt on macOS. The read now happens once, off the start-up path, on a blocking thread, into `ProviderRegistry`'s cache, and health reads the cache. That is the shape the rest of this task needs too: anything that touches a credential store is a job with progress rather than a command, which is exactly what `R10` says. **Owed:** the settings registry that holds enablement and priority (`PG-E20`), the settings screen, and the Discover flow that calls `build` and then `sync_search` — which is the first end-to-end path and needs the keyring read to be its own job. **What the 11.7 s prompt actually is, measured on a developer's machine rather than inferred (PG-E5-29's round):** `keyring` 4.2's v1 API resolves on macOS to `apple_native_keyring_store::keychain::Store` — the *legacy* login keychain, not the protected-data store — and a legacy item's access control names the application that created it. A rebuilt binary is a different application to the Keychain, so every rebuild of the dev watcher raises the permission dialog again, and the read blocks until somebody answers it. **The evidence that nothing is lost, from that machine:** the item was present (`security find-generic-password -s Peregrine -a provider.tmdb` answered in milliseconds with `cdat` from two days earlier and `mdat` from that same minute), two credential jobs and four title refreshes had succeeded minutes before, and the app's own log showed them. What the user saw was the Settings screen saying the key was not stored — so the two things to fix were the *reporting* (a refused or pending read is not an absent key, `CredentialStore`) and the *wait* (the screen read the status once, so a read that finished after the mount could never appear; it now polls for a bounded fifteen seconds and offers **Try again**). Not fixed, and named rather than implied: a release build keeps its signature, so the dialog is answered once and "Always Allow" sticks, while a development build re-asks on every rebuild — the dev-loop answer is a stable signing identity for local builds, which is packaging work and not a keyring one.
  - Acceptance: Diagnostics lists exactly the endpoints contacted
- [ ] **PG-E5-13** `L` · deps PG-E5-08, PG-E4-04 · Title matching engine: external ID resolution, alias and year reconciliation **The rule and its corpus landed.** `peregrine-domain/src/matching.rs` decides what a provider's candidate is, by three facts in order: an **external id is identity** (whatever either side is called), a **name and a year are evidence** (among locals of the same kind, matching by name *or* by an alias), and everything else is **new** — with the honest fourth answer, `Ambiguous`, for two locals that fit equally well, because guessing merges two titles or splits one and both lose data. A *known* year that differs is a remake; a year nobody recorded is not evidence, which is a correction the review below forced. There is **one normalization in the application** now: the library indexer's private `sort_name_of` was byte-identical to this one and now delegates, so a name read from a filename, typed by a person, and returned by a provider cannot differ in a way that matters. **The acceptance is a 127-case corpus** (`crates/peregrine-domain/tests/data/title_matching.json`, `MINIMUM_CASES = 100`) with **every** case agreeing, and its provenance is what makes that worth something: the *spec* was written first, the corpus was authored from the spec by a separate worker with no sight of the implementation, and the author machine-checked its own expectations against a re-implementation of the spec. **The spec was wrong twice**, both caught by the harness rather than by luck: it asked for a "candidate with no external id", which the port makes unrepresentable (`SearchCandidate::new` requires an `ExternalId`), and it named `anilist`, `omdb`, and `musicbrainz` as providers, none of which this build has — `anilist` is `PG-E5-14`'s owed addition. **An adversarial review of the rule found twelve things; four changed the code.** (1) A local whose year nobody recorded used to return `New` against a candidate that had a year — the asymmetry V1 found, and the case that would have duplicated the library's own stubs (indexed from a filename that stated no year) on the first sync that found them at a provider; the corpus case was updated and says why. (2) Two local rows with one id returned `Ambiguous { local_ids: [3, 3] }`; a caller reading `title JOIN title_alias` emits a title once per alias, and the same duplicate *within* one title was already treated as decidable, so duplicate ids are now one row. (3) A zero-width space made two identical-looking names compare different — the unicode-spoofing hazard §4 lists; U+200B/U+2060/U+FEFF are stripped and the joiners U+200C/U+200D are deliberately kept, because Arabic and Indic scripts use them to mean something. (4) A CJK assertion that could not fail was replaced with one that can. **A doc gap the review prompted:** the identity rule ignores `kind` on purpose, because I1's `UNIQUE (provider, value)` makes an external id unique across the whole table — one id cannot belong to a film and a series — and the module now says so where it was only implied. **Owed, recorded rather than half-fixed:** no Unicode normalization and no locale-aware casing, so `İSTANBUL` does not fold to `istanbul`, `STRASSE` does not fold to `Straße`, and a full-width spelling does not fold to its ASCII one — each is a normalization decision that belongs to a task of its own; `MatchBasis` is not persisted anywhere yet, so which basis a row was matched on is not auditable; and the rule has **no production caller** until the sync lands, which is the R3 tension the same review named — the corpus is its only caller today. Also owed from the tasks this depends on: `PG-E5-08`'s TV details, and `PG-E5-14`'s subtype and anime ordering, which will need `Candidate`/`LocalTitle` to carry a subtype.
  - Acceptance: 100-case matching corpus with expected outcomes

  - Acceptance: Anime fixtures classified correctly and ordered absolutely
- [ ] **PG-E5-15** `M` · deps PG-E5-13 · Artwork normalization into `image_ref` with language and vote metadata **The fetch half landed: the port reads images and the sync caches them.** `MetadataProvider::image(path, kind)` is the port's third operation; `ImageKind` (domain) says *which* picture (poster or backdrop) rather than a pixel size, because sizes are a provider's own naming and a domain type that named one provider's would change the day another numbered theirs differently — the adapter picks the size for the kind. **The credential is deliberately not sent to the image host:** TMDB serves images from a second, public host, and sending a key to it would hand the key to whoever runs that host, so an image request carries no credential and — per `AGENTS R24`'s documented exception — no conditional header either. `peregrine-sync::cache_image` puts the bytes in the content-addressed blob cache and answers with the path; `contains` is what avoids the second *request*, not just the second write. **The image base is a parameter**, for the same reason the API base is: an adapter that could only talk to the live CDN could not be tested at all (`R14`). **A test found a real defect here:** cleaning a provider-supplied path with `replace("..", "")` left a double slash (`/w342//poster.jpg`), which some servers serve and others refuse — the path is now cleaned a segment at a time, so `..` cannot climb out, `.` and empty segments cannot make a `//`, and a path starting with a scheme never gets the chance to replace the host. **Owed:** normalizing artwork into `image_ref` rows with language and vote metadata, the blurhash, and the eviction caller (`PG-E6-10`). The screen that shows a poster landed — `PG-E2-08`'s asset protocol in front of the cache, with the title read answering the cached path (`crates/peregrine-app/src/artwork.rs`). The fetch this task landed is what writes the blob that path names, and the sizes it writes and the size the read looks for are now one function, `ImageVariant::for_kind`, so they cannot drift.
  - Acceptance: Primary selection deterministic across fixtures

  - Acceptance: No fixture contains an API key; a leak check runs in CI

  - Acceptance: Adding an adapter requires no new test scaffolding

- [x] **PG-E5-20** `S` · deps PG-E5-06 · Key validation flow with a live "test connection" action
  - Acceptance: Invalid key produces an actionable error, not a generic failure
  - **Its first slice landed, and the acceptance is met at the layer that decides it.** `MetadataProvider` gained `check` — the port's one operation that is not one of ARCHITECTURE §13's eight, because it belongs to no capability: it asks whether the provider can be used at all. TMDB answers it against `/configuration`, the smallest request it has, and **the body is never decoded**: a build that failed on a configuration shape it does not read would report a working key as broken the day the provider adds a field. The distinction the task is named for is asserted directly — a 401 is `ProviderError::Unauthorized`, a 500 is `ProviderError::Transport`, and `a_broken_provider_is_not_reported_as_a_broken_credential` pins that they are not the same failure, because "that key was refused" and "TMDB could not be reached" are different instructions to a person. A fourth test asserts the credential travels as a bearer header and never as a query parameter, on the request the server actually received (SECURITY.md T13). The conformance suite now exercises the probe for **every** adapter, so a refusal attributed to a provider nobody asked fails the suite in the same way a misnamed search refusal does, with its own fixture and test.
  - **The service landed: `jobs/provider_credential.rs` and the runtime wiring.** One job per provider, with three actions — `Verify` proves a stored key still works, `Replace` proves a candidate and stores it, `Forget` removes what is stored — and **the rule the task is about is a named test**: `a_refused_credential_is_never_stored` runs a `Replace` against a fake that refuses and asserts the store is still empty, with the journal row carrying `PROVIDER_UNAUTHORIZED`. Three more rules are pinned the same way: a `Verify` with nothing stored answers `PROVIDER_NOT_CONFIGURED` **without building an adapter** (a probe must not be spent finding out there is nothing to test), an unavailable keyring is `PROVIDER_STORE` and not a generic failure, and a cancellation that arrives while the probe is in flight does not store the key — checked *after* the await, because an answer that arrived once the user stopped waiting must not write anything. The secret lives in the job's memory and is moved into the blocking write rather than cloned (`Secret` is deliberately not `Clone`), and two tests prove it never reaches the journal: one reads the row back, and one shuts the scheduler down so the row exists and the job never runs, then scans every database file's bytes for the key — both were checked by deliberately leaking the key and watching them fail.
  - **A contradiction this task forced into the open.** `NewJob::priority` documented "lower runs first" while `peregrine_sync`'s ready heap pops the **highest** priority and breaks ties by submission order — which is what `library_scan` and `hello` at 100 and `provider_refresh` at 10 already meant. A settings field somebody is waiting at would have queued *behind* a poster at priority 5. The credential job is 100, and `NewJob`'s doc is corrected in the same change rather than left to mislead the next reader.
  - **The interface landed: four commands and a Settings section.** `providers::status`, `providers::set_key`, `providers::test_connection` and `providers::clear_key` are the surface — each parses, starts one job and returns the job's identity, so no command touches the keyring and none of them can exceed the command budget (R10). The DTO carries the manifest's own display name and endpoint rather than a second table in the screen (R7), a whitespace-only key is refused with an instruction instead of stored, and an unknown provider is refused before a job row exists. Settings gained a **Providers** section: the field is a password input and is never read back, the outcome is followed through the existing `job://state` mechanism rather than a second one, and the failure code becomes one sentence through a pure mapping with its own tests — "That key was refused. Check you copied the API key and not another value." for `PROVIDER_UNAUTHORIZED`, and so on for rate-limited, unreachable and the store. A deliberate decision worth keeping: the **known** set comes from the manifest and only the *configured* flag is the cache's, because the keyring refresh is spawned off start-up — a cache-only answer would have claimed the build has no providers built in. **A second state on that same read, added after a reader asked why a reinstall appeared to delete their key:** the DTO now carries `CredentialStoreDto` (`unread`/`answered`/`denied`/`unavailable`) beside the provider list, and the registry records *why* a read ended rather than leaving a refusal looking like an empty store. The defect is a sentence and it is worth stating plainly: macOS binds a keychain item's access to the code signature of the application that created it, so a rebuilt, re-signed, or reinstalled binary is a different application to the Keychain and raises the prompt `PG-E5-12` measured — and a denial leaves every `configured` flag false exactly as a first run does, which is how "your key is in your keychain" was being shown as "No key stored". Three rules are now tests: a refusal is recorded as `denied` or `unavailable` and never as `answered` (`a_refused_read_is_recorded_as_a_refusal_and_not_as_an_empty_store`), a registry nobody has read answers `unread` rather than claiming nothing is configured, and a *later* refusal does not forget a provider that was read successfully — the adapters built from that key are still in hand, so the session can still ask. The screen adds one pure mapping (`providerStateKey`, `storeNoticeKey`) with its own tests, so a row can never say "No key stored" about a store nobody looked in, and the section says where the key lives: in the OS keychain, which is why reinstalling Peregrine does not remove it.
  - **Residual, and it is a human's half:** no run against the live service by the agent. Everything is proved against fixtures and the fakes — the port, the job, the commands, and the screen — but whether TMDB accepts a real key, and what its refusal actually looks like in the field, is unobserved here. It needs one person with a key to paste it into the new field; the evidence then is the keyring entry, the provider answering, and a rail filling with artwork.

  - Acceptance: ADR updated
- [ ] **PG-E5-22** `S` · deps PG-E5-03, PG-E25-02 · Rate-limit observability: remaining budget surfaced per provider
  - Acceptance: Diagnostics shows budget and last 429

## E6 · Sync and cache

- [ ] **PG-E6-01** `L` · deps PG-E3-08, PG-E4-19 · Job scheduler: priority queues, worker pool, dedupe, backoff with jitter **Remaining:** The scheduler runs behind the desktop runtime with a persisted job and IPC progress (PG-E0-19), and its queue overhead was measured once (about 55 000 no-op jobs/s in a debug build) — that number is recorded in the status section, not enforced. What remains: the shared testkit fakes (PG-E4-19), and the conformance suite a fake engine passes. **Marker cleared — nobody is on this task.** Its own note already names what remains, and the largest piece is the shared testkit fakes, which is PG-E4-19 and is in flight. **Two latent defects found while reading the crate for PG-E5-13's matching work, recorded here because this task owns the scheduler.** `Scheduler::submit_if_absent`'s doc promises `Ok(None)` when an existing job with the same key took the submission, but the body is `.map(Some)` over the enqueue, so `Ok(None)` is unreachable — a coalesced submission returns a handle that resolves `JobOutcome::Cancelled` instead, which is what `src/tests.rs` asserts. Either the doc or the signature is stale, and a caller that branches on `None` would never take that branch: a sync that keys its jobs by provider id and waits for `None` to mean "already running" would wait on the wrong thing. And `SchedulerError::Engine` has no constructor anywhere in the crate, so that variant cannot be produced by any failure — an error a caller handles and nothing raises. **That coalesce path is now reachable from a screen, and that is worth adding to this task's list:** `Runtime::start_provider_refresh` inserts a journal row *before* it asks the scheduler, and the title screen asks once per visit — so opening a title, leaving, and coming back while its refresh is still running submits a second job with the same dedupe key. The second submission coalesces onto the first, the loser's handle resolves `Cancelled` with no state change, and the second journal row stays `queued` for ever while the screen's follow never clears. It recovers on the next visit, and nothing is lost, but a stuck `queued` row is a lie in a surface `PG-E25-07` is meant to make trustworthy. `PG-E14-04` reached the same defect and worked around it by not deduping scans at all; the fix here is the one that note asks for — "a handle the engine has given up on should say so" — rather than another workaround. **A third, found while putting a title screen in front of `jobs::provider_refresh` and owned here rather than fixed there:** a *failed* job reports nothing at all. A job publishes a terminal `job://state` only on the paths it writes itself (`hello` publishes `Succeeded` and `Canceled`, `library_scan` the same, and `provider_refresh` — since this round — `Succeeded` on both of its success paths); a failure is the *attempt's* outcome, the scheduler may retry, so no job can report it, and nothing on the completion path does either — the runtime keeps its `JobHandle` in `live` for cancellation and never awaits it. So `job://state` has no terminal event for a failed job, and every screen that follows one (`useScan` today, `useTitle` now) waits for a stop that never arrives. **The journal is the worse half and the same cause:** `JobChange::Finished` is written by the job, so a job that fails leaves its row `running` for good — the Diagnostics screen shows a perpetually running job — and the `Deferred` a retry schedules is written by nobody, so a retry is invisible in the history `PG-E25-07` is meant to draw. Fixing it means observing a job's resolution and writing the phase it ended in, which is a change to the runtime's job bookkeeping and not to a screen; the title screen was written so that nothing visible depends on it — the follow is internal and a poster that never arrives is the same answer the failure produces —  **A fourth, found by watching the shipping app instead of the suite, and fixed in this round:** a row a *killed* process left non-terminal holds its dedupe key for ever, because `job_dedupe_uq` is a partial unique index over exactly the non-terminal rows. The evidence was on this machine: journal row 25 sat `queued` after the dev watcher restarted the shell mid-fetch, `Runtime::start_discover_rails` joined it on every later launch (its log line repeats `the discover rails job is already running {journal_id: 25}`), `insert_job` could not write a second row for the key, and pressing Refresh went through the same join — so Discover fetched nothing, for ever, and the *manifest* this round added stayed empty for the same reason. `crate::jobs::fail_unfinished` now closes every non-terminal row when the database is opened, with `last_error_code = 'INTERRUPTED'` and a reason; a job cannot outlive the process that was running it, and the journal cannot tell the rows of a process that died from the rows of one still working, so open time is the only honest moment to decide. What that does *not* cover is the same stuck row produced by a coalesce *inside* one process, which is the defect above: that one needs the loser's handle to report its resolution, and it is still owed here.
  - Acceptance: Deferred jobs retry on schedule; dedupe proven

  - Acceptance: Injected panic releases the token (guard test)

  - Acceptance: Each class has an independent limit asserted under load
- [ ] **PG-E6-04** `M` · deps PG-E6-01, PG-E5-09 · Incremental change sync with persisted cursors and overlapping windows **The sync's first half landed ahead of this task's cursors.** `peregrine-sync::catalogue::sync_search` asks a provider, decides what the catalogue already holds with PG-E5-13's rule, and writes the rest; `Database::upsert_provider_title` finds-or-creates-and-attaches in **one transaction**, which is the orphan-stub race the storage recon found (`title` has no UNIQUE constraint, so a read-then-insert left a stub nothing points at); and `local_titles_for_sort_name` gives the rule all the locals a name could be, with their aliases and ids. Two tests cover the acceptance's real question — syncing twice makes one title. Owed here still: persisted cursors, overlapping windows, and crash-safety between cursor advance and upsert.
  - Acceptance: Crash between cursor advance and upsert loses nothing

- [ ] **PG-E6-09** `L` · deps PG-E2-08, PG-E3-09 · Image cache: content-addressed store, variant matrix, atomic writes **The blob store landed; the rows and the cap did not.** `peregrine-sync::images` holds `ImageCache`, `ImageKey`, and `ImageVariant`: the key is `sha256(provider|path|variant)` as lowercase hex, exactly the value `DATA_MODEL`'s `image_cache.key` column documents, verified against `printf 'tmdb|abc|w342' | shasum -a 256` rather than against a remembered constant. Byte-level JSON is the `ImageVariant` pair the research recommends keeping — a `w342` poster and a `w780` backdrop — with everything else proxied, and the provider's full size list deliberately **not** enumerated, because TMDB's comes from `/3/configuration` at runtime and a table here would be a second copy of something the provider owns. **The acceptance is met and the test states it the way a test can:** a blob only ever appears under its final name by `rename` after `write_all` and `sync_all`, so a crash leaves either no file or the whole file, and the file a killed download leaves behind has the `partial` suffix and is therefore never a hit — the suite stages that leftover and asserts the cache reports the image as absent. The directory is deliberately **not** fsynced: without it a crash can lose the rename and the image is fetched again, which is a cost this cache can afford and a partial poster is not. **The name "content-addressed" is loose and worth stating:** two providers serving byte-identical artwork get two blobs, because the key hashes the identity and not the bytes; what it buys is a filesystem-safe name two writers agree on, and a hostile provider path cannot escape the directory because no part of the path reaches the filesystem at all. **The rows landed too**, in `peregrine-storage`'s `image_cache` module: record, read, touch, remove, and one totals query. The two halves are written by different layers and neither can be reached from the other, so the relationship is stated where it belongs rather than assumed: **a row without a file is a row an eviction tolerates**, and **a file without a row is a leak** that only the cache wipe (`PG-E6-18`) reaps, because eviction walks rows and only a directory walk finds what the rows do not know about. That is why the row is written *after* the bytes are published — a crash between the two loses a file rather than recording bytes that are not there — and why `record` is an upsert that keeps `created_at` and `hits`: a row that already exists is the same image rediscovered after a row loss or a restored backup, not a new file and not a read. A read is `touch`, which the *caller* reports, because only the caller knows whether the file it found was the one it was looking for. **One query, two callers:** the artwork tier of `DATA_MODEL` §7 already summed this column, so the totals live in the module and the usage report now calls them instead of holding its own `SELECT` — two copies would have drifted, and the copy that drifted would have been the one nobody tested. **Still owed, and owned by PG-E6-10:** the least-recently-used *ordering* and the byte cap's policy. The ordering is deliberately absent here: `PG-E6-10` owns that query and therefore the index that serves it (AGENTS §5), and adding one now would be an index for a read nobody has written. Also owed by PG-E6-10's own list, and stated in the module: eviction removes the file first and the row second, the same order the record is written in and for the same reason. **The reader landed with the screen.** A key is turned into something the webview can load by the *title read* rather than by a command of its own: `peregrine-app`'s `artwork::cached_image` hashes the provider's path with `ImageVariant::for_kind` — the same mapping the fetch uses, moved into one function so a cache that writes one size and looks for another is not expressible — and answers with the blob's path only when the file is there, because a row without a file is a row an eviction tolerates and a path whose bytes are gone is a path the asset protocol refuses. `get_title` carries it as `TitleDetailDto.poster`, and `TitleArtwork.tsx` is the one place it becomes an `<img>`. **A gap found while putting a screen in front of the cache, and recorded rather than fixed here:** nothing in the shipping binary writes an `image_cache` row. `Database::record_image`, `touch_image`, `remove_image`, and `pin_image` are called from `peregrine-storage`'s own tests and from nowhere else, and `sync::cache_image` publishes the bytes without recording them — so the relationship this task states ("the row is written *after* the bytes are published") holds for a writer that does not exist yet. The read path added with the title screen does not need the row, because it asks the filesystem whether the blob is there, so the visible consequence today is the accounting and the cap rather than a missing poster: `PG-E6-10`'s eviction walks rows, and a cache whose rows are never written cannot be evicted to its byte cap at all. It belongs to this task because this task owns the row, and it is not fixed here because the eviction caller that would consume the rows is `PG-E6-10`'s and unfixed too — recording rows with nothing to evict them would be half of an integration. **The fetch path's own reporting was repaired in the same round that put a screen in front of it, and for that reason.** Three things were wrong with the job as it landed, all of them invisible while nothing called it: it never wrote a terminal journal row, so its row said `running` for ever; it published no state event on the "nothing to fill in" path, which is the *common* one for a freshly scanned library, so a screen following it waited for a stop that never came; and it asked the first *configured* provider rather than the one owning an identifier the title carries, so a title whose id belongs to a second provider would have been asked for and answered `` `None` `` without a request. The row is now closed with `JobChange::Finished { Succeeded }` (after the progress write, because `Finished` does not touch `progress_permille` and a row left at the last reported fraction shows a finished job as three-fifths done), every success publishes the terminal phase, and the adapters are walked until one owns an id. **What is still owed here, and now has a caller that can reach it:** a title nobody can be asked about — no configured provider holds an id for it — is answered `refresh_due = false` by `crates/peregrine-app/src/artwork.rs`, so the screen does not start a job at all. **The fetch path exists and runs as a job.** `peregrine-app::jobs::provider_refresh` fills one title from its provider and then fetches its poster into the blob cache, in two reported phases. A job rather than a command because it leaves the machine twice (`ARCHITECTURE §7` rule 2): a screen that blocked on a provider having a slow day would be a screen that froze. It is keyed by the title, so opening the same screen twice waits on one refresh rather than spending two requests on one answer, and a provider with no artwork path is a fact rather than a failure — the job succeeds with the metadata it has. `sync::refresh_title` now returns the details it wrote, because the caller needs the artwork paths they carry and a second read to learn what was just written is a second read for nothing. `Runtime::start_provider_refresh` and the `refresh_title` command expose it. **Owed:** thumbnail variants and the blurhash, and the eviction caller (`PG-E6-10`). The screen landed with the reader — a poster on `PG-E7-11`'s title page, over `PG-E2-08`'s protocol — and the *fetch* it needed has a caller now: the read carries `refreshDue`, a rule the core computes (`crates/peregrine-app/src/artwork.rs`), and the title screen asks for one refresh per visit when it is true. **A defect found while wiring that up, and recorded rather than fixed here:** the asset protocol was scoped to `<OS cache directory>/images` while this cache was rooted at `<data directory>/artwork`, so no blob it wrote could ever be served; the directory is now one value the shell resolves and gives to both (see `PG-E2-08`). **One finding for whoever touches the command budget next:** `jobs::command_tests::no_command_blocks_for_longer_than_the_budget` failed once in a full parallel run with `system::health` at 11.7 s and passed on both re-runs — that exact number is the macOS keyring prompt, so the failure was the health command reading a cache that was cold because the startup refresh had not finished, not a regression in this change. It is a real flake worth fixing: the test asserts a *first* call inside a budget the first call cannot meet. **The rule's last blind spot was a series, and it is closed.** "Filled in" was read off `metadata_state`, which only ever records the *details* read — a series is filled in two reads, and the seasons are the second one. So a show whose details landed before the seasons slice existed, or whose season read failed after the details were written, was a show with no rows in `season` that no visit would ever ask about again: measured against a real library, 62 of 635 shows were in exactly that state and only 22 had seasons. `title.season_synced_at` (migration 0019) is that fact, written by `refresh_title` only *after* the season step answers and never before it, and `refresh_due` now asks when a show's `Seasons` is `NeverRead`. The same migration repairs what 0014 left behind — a `partial` row with none of the nine details-only columns was stamped by a list answer and claims a read nobody did — and backfills the column for every show that already holds a season. The screen's half is `PG-E7-13`'s: the read that runs when the page opens answers nothing on a first visit, so it now runs again when the refresh it asked for stops. **A bounded bulk backfill was considered and rejected rather than skipped:** the credential read that decides whether any provider can be asked runs off the startup path, so there is no reliable launch-time hook; and the population left after the repair is a handful of shows whose season lists run to dozens of requests each, which is a spend `AGENTS R24` asks not to make for pages nobody opened. Every stuck show now heals on its own open, which is the visit that was broken.
  - Acceptance: No partial file survives a crash (temp + rename)
- [x] **PG-E6-10** `M` · deps PG-E6-09 · Image LRU eviction to the configured cap with pinned-item protection **Done, both halves of the acceptance proven by named tests.** `peregrine_domain::cache`'s `plan_eviction` is the policy: least recently used first, the key breaking a tie so the same cache always yields the same plan, and a pinned entry skipped entirely. `pinned` needed a column — nothing in the table said an image was *in use*, and `last_used_at` says when an image was last read rather than whether something is holding it now, so a policy that guessed from recency would evict the poster on screen the moment the user stopped scrolling. It is a flag rather than a reference count: a count would have to be decremented on every path, including the ones that end in a panic or a cancelled download, and a leaked count is an image that can never be evicted — a worse failure than evicting one that had just stopped being used. **A cap the pinned entries alone exceed is reported rather than broken** (`cap_unreachable`): pinned entries are in use, and a policy that evicted one to satisfy a number would be trading a correctness guarantee for a target. Migration 0013 adds the column and a **partial** index over `(last_used_at, key) WHERE pinned = 0` — partial because the rows an in-use image leaves behind are the ones the policy may not touch and the ones the order does not need to carry, and deliberately not 0007's `image_cache_lru_idx`, which covers `last_used_at` alone and would still have to sort. **Tested at the layer that can break it:** `eviction_takes_the_least_recently_used_rows_until_the_cap_is_met`, `eviction_never_touches_an_image_in_use`, `a_cap_the_pinned_rows_alone_exceed_is_reported_not_broken`, and `unpinning_lets_the_next_run_evict_what_it_kept` — a pin that could not be released would be a leak the cap never recovers from. **Two things are owed by other tasks and are not pretended here:** the *trigger* — the pass is a maintenance-scale read of the whole cache, which `PG-E3-16`'s scheduler runs, and the plan's steps are where it belongs — and the blob on disk, which `PG-E6-09`'s fetch path writes and which eviction must delete alongside the row — that path exists now, so the row is no longer the whole of the cache and the two can disagree, in the direction `PG-E6-09` documents: a row without a file is tolerated, a file without a row is a leak only a cache wipe reaps. Migration 0013 also cost four fixes rather than ten, because `PG-E14-09`'s round made the migration-count tests derive the set instead of hardcoding it.

- [ ] **PG-E6-15** `S` · deps PG-E6-04 · Stale-metadata policy, refresh-on-view, and the TMDB six-month cache compliance sweep **Part of it now exists, and the boundary matters:** `crates/peregrine-app/src/artwork.rs`'s `refresh_due` answers "no provider has ever filled this title in, a provider is known to have artwork the cache does not hold, or a show's seasons have never been read", and `get_title` carries it so the title screen asks once per visit. That is *not* a staleness policy: it has no window, no `MetadataState::Stale` handling, and no opinion about a title whose metadata is merely old — a filled title with its poster on disk is one it says not to ask about, however old the fill is. The six-month compliance sweep and the state machine that replaces this flag are still this task's, and whoever lands it should delete the flag rather than layer a second rule beside it.
